\displaystyle=\sum_{k\geq 1}\langle S_{1,k}({X^{(1)}})-S_{2,k}({X^{(2)}}),\,\Delta\rangle.

We first determine the order of
\langle S_{1,1}({X^{(1)}}),\,\Delta\rangle
.
Consider the scalar quantity

T:=\tr\big(\Delta\,\beta_{1}^{-1}{X^{(1)}}\beta_{1}^{\perp}\big).

By cyclicity of the trace,

T=\tr\big(\beta_{1}^{\perp}\Delta\beta_{1}^{-1}{X^{(1)}}\big)=\langle W,\,{X^{(1)}}\rangle,

where

W:=\beta_{1}^{\perp}\Delta\beta_{1}^{-1}.

Expanding over unordered indices gives

T=\sum_{p}W_{pp}X_{pp}+\sum_{p<q}(W_{pq}+W_{qp})X_{pq}

with

a_{pp}=W_{pp},a_{pq}=W_{pq}+W_{qp}(p<q).

Applying Bernstein’s inequality to the independent, mean–zero summands
a_{pq}X_{pq}
yields

\mathbb{P}(|T|\geq u)\leq 2\exp\Big(-\frac{u^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}+\tfrac{2}{3}Mu}\Big),

where

\sigma^{2}=\sum_{p\leq q}\Var(X_{pq})a_{pq}^{2},
and

M=\max_{p\leq q}|a_{pq}|
.
Since
\Var(X_{pq})\lesssim\rho_{n}
and
\sum_{p\leq q}a_{pq}^{2}\asymp\|W\|_{F}^{2}
, we have

\sigma^{2}\lesssim\rho_{n}\|W\|_{F}^{2}.
By submultiplicativity of norms,

\|W\|_{F}=\|\beta_{1}^{\perp}\Delta\beta_{1}^{-1}\|_{F}\lesssim\frac{\|\Delta\|_{F}}{n\rho_{n}},

which gives

\sigma^{2}\lesssim\rho_{n}\Big(\frac{\|\Delta\|_{F}}{n\rho_{n}}\Big)^{2}=\frac{\|\Delta\|_{F}^{2}}{n^{2}\rho_{n}},
and

M\leq\|W\|_{F}\lesssim\frac{\|\Delta\|_{F}}{n\rho_{n}}.

Choosing
u=C\sigma\sqrt{\log n}
with a sufficiently large constant
C
,
we obtain
T=O_{p}(\sigma\sqrt{\log n})
. Hence,

\displaystyle\langle S_{1,1}({X^{(1)}}),\,\Delta\rangle=O_{p}\left(\frac{\|\Delta\|_{F}\sqrt{\log n}}{n\sqrt{\rho}_{n}}\right),

(192)

which implies that

\displaystyle\langle S_{1,1}({X^{(1)}})-S_{2,1}({X^{(2)}}),\,\Delta\rangle=O_{p}\left(\frac{\|\Delta\|_{F}\sqrt{\rho_{n}\log n}}{n\rho_{n}}\right).

For higher–order terms (
l\geq 2
), Theorem 1 of
58
implies

\bigg|\langle S_{i,l}({X^{(i)}}),\,\Delta\rangle\bigg|\leq\|S_{i,l}({X^{(i)}})\|_{F}\|\Delta\|_{F}\lesssim\left(\frac{\|X^{(i)}\|}{n\rho_{n}}\right)^{l}\|\Delta\|_{F}=O_{p}\left(\frac{\|\Delta\|_{F}}{(n\rho_{n})^{l/2}}\right).

Combining the sharp Bernstein bound for the first–order term from
Eq.

192
with the rate for higher–order terms yields

L(\Delta)=O_{p}\left(\max\left(\frac{\|\Delta\|_{F}}{n\rho_{n}},\frac{\|\Delta\|_{F}\sqrt{\rho_{n}\log n}}{n\rho_{n}}\right)\right).

∎

Proof of
Lemma

20

Proof.

Because
\col(P)=\col(Z)
and
\rank(P)=k
, the
k
nonzero population eigenvectors of
P
span the column space of
Z
. Hence there exists a full-rank matrix
W\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times k}
such that

V=ZW.

By construction each row of
Z
is a standard basis vector: if node
i
belongs to community
a
then the
i
-th row of
Z
equals
e_{a}^{\top}
. Therefore the rows of
V
are constant on communities, namely for any node
i
in community
a
we have

V_{i,\cdot}=W_{a,\cdot}.

Orthonormality of the columns of
V
gives

I_{k}=V^{\top}V=W^{\top}Z^{\top}Z\,W=:W^{\top}NW.

Define
U:=N^{1/2}W
. Then

U^{\top}U=W^{\top}NW=I_{k},

so
U
is a
k\times k
orthonormal matrix. Consequently

W=N^{-1/2}U.

Examining a row
a
of
W
yields

\|W_{a,\cdot}\|=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n_{a}}}\,\|U_{a,\cdot}\|.

Since
U
is square and orthonormal, each row of
U
has Euclidean norm equal to
1
. Hence for every community
a
,

\|W_{a,\cdot}\|=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n_{a}}}.

Therefore for any node
i
in community
a
,

\|V_{i,\cdot}\|=\|W_{a,\cdot}\|=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n_{a}}}.

Under the balanced-size assumption
n_{a}\asymp\,n/k
we obtain

\|V\|_{2,\infty}\asymp\sqrt{\frac{k}{c\,n}}.

∎

Proof of
Lemma

21

Proof.

The proof follows the same high-level steps as
Lemma

20
for the SBM. Because
\col(P)=\col(Z)
and
\rank(P)=k
, there exists a full–rank matrix
W\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times k}
such that

V=ZW.

Orthonormality of the columns of
V
yields the same relation

I_{k}=W^{\top}NW.

Setting
U:=N^{1/2}W
we have

W=N^{-1/2}U
implying
V=ZN^{-1/2}U.
Right multiplication by the orthonormal matrix
U
preserves row Euclidean norms. Therefore for each node
i
,

\|V_{i,\cdot}\|=\|\,Z_{i,\cdot}N^{-1/2}U\,\|=\|\,Z_{i,\cdot}N^{-1/2}\,\|.
By the operator norm inequality,

\|V_{i,\cdot}\|\leq\|Z_{i,\cdot}\|\,\|N^{-1/2}\|=\frac{\|Z_{i,\cdot}\|}{\sqrt{\lambda_{\min}(N)}}.

Since each row
Z_{i,\cdot}
is a probability vector we have
\|Z_{i,\cdot}\|\leq\|Z_{i,\cdot}\|_{1}=1
, and thus

\|V_{i,\cdot}\|\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{\lambda_{\min}(N)}}
for all
i.
Finally applying the assumed spectral bound
\lambda_{\min}(N)\geq c_{1}\,n/k
completes the proof.
∎

Appendix I
Proof of
Propositions

1
,
2
,
3
and
4
and
Theorem

2

Proof of
Proposition

1

Proof.

Since
V^{(i)}
contains the eigenvectors corresponding to the non-zero eigenvalues of
P^{(i)}
, we have
\col(V^{(i)})=\col(P^{(i)})
.
Substituting the structure of the GRDPG probability matrix, we have

\col(P^{(i)})=\col(\Gamma^{(i)}I_{p_{i},q_{i}}{\Gamma^{(i)}}^{\top}).

I_{p_{i},q_{i}}
is a diagonal matrix with entries
\pm 1
and hence, is non-singular. Furthermore, because
\Gamma^{(i)}
has full column rank
k
, the product
I_{p_{i},q_{i}}{\Gamma^{(i)}}^{\top}
has full row rank
k
. Right-multiplication by a full row-rank matrix preserves the column space of the left matrix. Therefore,

\col(P^{(i)})=\col(\Gamma^{(i)}).

Consequently, the condition
V^{(1)}{V^{(1)}}^{\top}=V^{(2)}{V^{(2)}}^{\top}
holds if and only if
\col(\Gamma^{(1)})=\col(\Gamma^{(2)})
.
For two full-rank matrices
\Gamma^{(1)},\Gamma^{(2)}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
, their column spaces are identical if and only if there exists an invertible matrix
W\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times k}
such that
\Gamma^{(1)}=\Gamma^{(2)}W
.
∎

Proof of
Proposition

2

Proof.

First note that, for each
i
,
\col(P^{(i)})\subseteq\col({Z^{(i)}})
because
P^{(i)}={Z^{(i)}}B^{(i)}{Z^{(i)}}^{\top}
. Since
B^{(i)}
is full-rank and
\rank(P^{(i)})=k
by assumption, we must have
\rank({Z^{(i)}})=k
and therefore

\col(P^{(i)})=\col({Z^{(i)}})
for
i=1,2.

If
{V^{(1)}}{V^{(1)}}^{\top}={V^{(2)}}{V^{(2)}}^{\top}
, then the two projection (equivalently column) spaces coincide:

\col({P^{(1)}})=\col({V^{(1)}})=\col({V^{(2)}})=\col({P^{(2)}}).

Using the above equation, this is equivalent to
\col(Z^{(1)})=\col(Z^{(2)})
. Conversely, if
\col(Z^{(1)})=\col(Z^{(2)}),
then
\col({P^{(1)}})=\col({P^{(2)}})
and hence
{V^{(1)}}{V^{(1)}}^{\top}={V^{(2)}}{V^{(2)}}^{\top}
. Because
\col(Z^{(1)})=\col(Z^{(2)})
and both
Z^{(1)}
and
Z^{(2)}
have full column rank, there exists an invertible
k\times k
matrix
R
such that

Z^{(2)}=Z^{(1)}R.

For the SBM, each canonical basis vector
e_{j}^{\top}
appears as a row of
Z^{(1)}
; applying
R
yields that the rows of
Z^{(2)}
are the vectors
e_{j}^{\top}R
. But each
e_{j}^{\top}R
must itself be a canonical basis vector (since rows of
Z^{(2)}
are one-hot); hence
R
sends the standard basis to the standard basis and therefore
R
is a permutation matrix. Thus
Z^{(2)}=Z^{(1)}\Pi
for some permutation
\Pi
, i.e., the membership matrices coincide up to relabeling of community indices.

For the MMSBM case, consider the pure rows of
Z^{(1)}
. If
i_{a}
is an index such that
Z^{(1)}_{i_{a}\cdot}=e_{a}^{\top}
, then evaluating
Z^{(2)}=Z^{(1)}R
at row
i_{a}
yields

Z^{(2)}_{i_{a}\cdot}=e_{a}^{\top}R.

This implies that the rows of
R
exactly coincide with the rows of
Z^{(2)}
corresponding to the pure nodes of
Z^{(1)}
. Because the rows of
Z^{(2)}
lie in the
(k-1)
-simplex, every row of
R
must also reside in the simplex; in particular,
R
is element-wise non-negative and its rows sum to one.

Z^{(2)}
also contains pure nodes for each community. Since
R
is invertible, we can express the latent position relationship as
Z^{(1)}=Z^{(2)}R^{-1}
. Evaluating this equation at the pure nodes of
Z^{(2)}
demonstrates that the rows of
R^{-1}
similarly correspond to rows of
Z^{(1)}
, which implies that
R^{-1}
is also an element-wise non-negative matrix.

Theorem 5.1 of
24
establishes that a matrix and its inverse are both non-negative if and only if the matrix can be expressed as the product of a diagonal matrix with strictly positive diagonal entries and a permutation matrix. This result implies that
R
must be a generalized permutation matrix. Because we have already established that the rows of
R
must sum to one, its non-zero entries are forced to be exactly one. Hence,
R
is a standard permutation matrix, and consequently,
Z^{(2)}=Z^{(1)}\Pi
for some permutation
\Pi
.
∎

Proof of
Proposition

3

Proof.

Write
B=\rho_{n}\widetilde{B}
and
N=Z^{\top}Z
. Then

P=ZBZ^{\top}=\rho_{n}\,Z\widetilde{B}Z^{\top}.

The nonzero eigenvalues of
Z\widetilde{B}Z^{\top}
coincide with the eigenvalues of the
k\times k
matrix

\widetilde{B}^{1/2}N\widetilde{B}^{1/2},

so that for each
1\leq j\leq k
,

\lambda_{j}(P)=\rho_{n}\cdot\lambda_{j}\big(\widetilde{B}^{1/2}N\widetilde{B}^{1/2}\big).

Suppose
v_{j}
is the eigenvector of
\widetilde{B}^{1/2}N\widetilde{B}^{1/2}
corresponding to the eigenvalue
\lambda_{j}\big(\widetilde{B}^{1/2}N\widetilde{B}^{1/2}\big).
Since
\widetilde{B}
and
N
are symmetric positive definite, we have

\displaystyle\lambda_{j}\big(\widetilde{B}^{1/2}N\widetilde{B}^{1/2}\big)

\displaystyle=v_{j}^{\top}\big(\widetilde{B}^{1/2}N\widetilde{B}^{1/2}\big)v_{j}.

which yields

\displaystyle\lambda_{\min}(N)\|\widetilde{B}^{1/2}v_{j}\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\lambda_{j}\big(\widetilde{B}^{1/2}N\widetilde{B}^{1/2}\big)\leq\lambda_{\max}(N)\|\widetilde{B}^{1/2}v_{j}\|^{2},

and further bounding
\|\widetilde{B}^{1/2}v_{j}\|^{2}
gives

\displaystyle\lambda_{\min}(N)\lambda_{\min}(\widetilde{B})

\displaystyle\leq\lambda_{j}\big(\widetilde{B}^{1/2}N\widetilde{B}^{1/2}\big)\leq\lambda_{\max}(N)\lambda_{\max}(\widetilde{B}).

Combining this result with
1
, the eigenvalue bounds on
B
, and the assumption of balanced community sizes (which imply there exist constants
b_{1},b_{2}>0
such that
b_{1}n\lesssim\lambda_{\min}(N)\leq\lambda_{\max}(N)\lesssim b_{2}n
), we obtain

\displaystyle a_{1}b_{1}\,n

\displaystyle\leq\lambda_{j}\big(\widetilde{B}^{1/2}N\widetilde{B}^{1/2}\big)\leq a_{2}b_{2}\,n.

This implies that

\displaystyle a_{1}b_{1}\,n\rho_{n}

\displaystyle\leq\lambda_{j}(P)\leq a_{2}b_{2}\,n\rho_{n},

which upon dividing by
n\rho_{n}
yields

\displaystyle a_{1}b_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\lambda_{j}(P)}{n\rho_{n}}\leq a_{2}b_{2},

completing the proof.
∎

Proof of
Theorem

2

Proof.

The conclusion follows directly from
Theorem

1
together with
Lemma

8
.
The latter establishes that

\widetilde{\sigma}_{2}^{2}\asymp k^{2}/(n^{3}\rho_{n}^{2})
.
Moreover, under
2′
we have

\frac{k}{n^{3/2}\rho_{n}}\gg\frac{k}{n^{2}\rho_{n}^{2}},

which, in combination with the preceding variance characterization, yields the desired result.
∎

Proof of
Proposition

4

We will break down the proof into several steps.

I.5.1
Step 1: Initial Decomposition.

Assume that
A
belongs to
\mathcal{E}_{{\sf very\ good}}
as defined in
Lemma

12
.
We slightly refine the proof of
Theorem

6
where it is already shown that
|\widehat{\mu}_{1}-\mu_{1}|=O_{p}\left(\max\left(\frac{k}{n^{2}\rho_{n}^{2}},\frac{k\sqrt{\log n}}{n^{2}\rho_{n}^{3/2}}\right)\right).
We will now show that
|\widehat{\mu}_{1}-\mu_{1}|\asymp\max\left(\frac{k}{n^{2}\rho_{n}^{2}},\frac{k\sqrt{\log n}}{n^{2}\rho_{n}^{3/2}}\right).
We will decompose
\mu_{1}^{(2)}
and
\widehat{\mu}_{1}^{(2)}
(defined in
Section

C.3.1
) in a slightly different way for this. We have that

\displaystyle\mu_{1}^{(2)}-\widehat{\mu}_{1}^{(2)}

\displaystyle=\tr\left(V\Lambda^{-2}V^{\top}\Diag(\Sigma\cdot d)-V\Lambda^{-2}V^{\top}\Diag(\Sigma\cdot\mathbf{1}_{n})\right)

(193)

\displaystyle+\tr\left(V\Lambda^{-2}V^{\top}\Diag(\Sigma\cdot\mathbf{1}_{n})-\widehat{V}\widehat{\Lambda}^{-2}\widehat{V}^{\top}\Diag(\widehat{\Sigma}\cdot\mathbf{1}_{n})\right)

\displaystyle+\tr\left(\widehat{V}\widehat{\Lambda}^{-2}\widehat{V}^{\top}\Diag(\widehat{\Sigma}\cdot\mathbf{1}_{n})-\widehat{V}\widehat{\Lambda}^{-2}\widehat{V}^{\top}\Diag(\widehat{\Sigma}\cdot\widehat{d})\right).

All the terms of
Eq.

193
except the second one
\tr\left(V\Lambda^{-2}V^{\top}\Diag(\Sigma\cdot\mathbf{1}_{n})-\widehat{V}\widehat{\Lambda}^{-2}\widehat{V}^{\top}\Diag(\widehat{\Sigma}\cdot\mathbf{1}_{n})\right)
have already been shown to be
o_{p}\left(\max\left(\frac{k}{n^{2}\rho_{n}^{2}},\frac{k\sqrt{\log n}}{n^{2}\rho_{n}^{3/2}}\right)\right)
in the proof of
Theorem

6
in
Section

C.3.1
.
Our target will be to prove that

\tr\left(V\Lambda^{-2}V^{\top}\,\Diag(P\cdot\mathbf{1}_{n})-\widehat{V}\widehat{\Lambda}^{-2}\widehat{V}^{\top}\,\Diag({\widehat{P}}\cdot\mathbf{1}_{n})\right)\asymp\max\left(\frac{k}{n^{2}\rho_{n}^{2}},\frac{k\sqrt{\log n}}{n^{2}\rho_{n}^{3/2}}\right),

because we can show that

\tr\left(V\Lambda^{-2}V^{\top}\,\Diag((P\circ P)\cdot\mathbf{1}_{n})-\widehat{V}\widehat{\Lambda}^{-2}\widehat{V}^{\top}\,\Diag(({\widehat{P}}\circ{\widehat{P}})\cdot\mathbf{1}_{n})\right)=o_{p}\left(\max\left(\frac{k}{n^{2}\rho_{n}^{2}},\frac{k\sqrt{\log n}}{n^{2}\rho_{n}^{3/2}}\right)\right)

by an argument entirely analogous to that used earlier in the proof of
Lemma

14
in
Section

G.3
.

We have that

\displaystyle\tr\left(V\Lambda^{-2}V^{\top}\,\Diag(P\cdot\mathbf{1}_{n})-\widehat{V}\widehat{\Lambda}^{-2}\widehat{V}^{\top}\,\Diag({\widehat{P}}\cdot\mathbf{1}_{n})\right)

\displaystyle=\tr\left(\left(V\Lambda^{-2}V^{\top}-\widehat{V}\widehat{\Lambda}^{-2}\widehat{V}^{\top}\right)\,\Diag(P\cdot\mathbf{1}_{n})\right)

(194)

\displaystyle-\tr\left(\left(\widehat{V}\widehat{\Lambda}^{-2}\widehat{V}^{\top}-V\Lambda^{-2}V^{\top}\right)\,\Diag(({\widehat{P}}-P)\cdot\mathbf{1}_{n})\right)

\displaystyle-\tr\!\left(V\Lambda^{-2}V^{\top}\,\Diag(({\widehat{P}}-P)\cdot\mathbf{1}_{n})\right).

We now expand the terms in
Eq.

194
one by one using
Lemma

13
,
Eq.

147
, and Theorem 1 of
58
. This yields the following decompositions:

\displaystyle\tr\bigg(

\displaystyle\bigl(\widehat{V}\widehat{\Lambda}^{-2}\widehat{V}^{\top}-V\Lambda^{-2}V^{\top}\bigr)\,\Diag(P\cdot\mathbf{1}_{n})\bigg)

(195)

\displaystyle=\tr\Big(\bigl[V\Lambda^{-2}V^{\top}S_{1}(X)+S_{1}(X)V\Lambda^{-2}V^{\top}-V\Lambda^{-2}V^{\top}(XP+PX)V\Lambda^{-2}V^{\top}\bigr]\Diag(P\cdot\mathbf{1}_{n})\Big)

\displaystyle+\tr\Big(S_{1}(X)\bigl(\widehat{V}\widehat{\Lambda}^{-2}\widehat{V}^{\top}-V\Lambda^{-2}V^{\top}\bigr)\Diag(P\cdot\mathbf{1}_{n})\Big)

\displaystyle-\tr\Big(V\Lambda^{-2}V^{\top}(XP+PX)\bigl(\widehat{V}\widehat{\Lambda}^{-2}\widehat{V}^{\top}-V\Lambda^{-2}V^{\top}\bigr)\Diag(P\cdot\mathbf{1}_{n})\Big)

\displaystyle+\tr\Big(\bigl(\sum_{j\geq 2}V\Lambda^{-2}V^{\top}S_{j}(X)+\sum_{j\geq 2}S_{j}(X)\widehat{V}\widehat{\Lambda}^{-2}\widehat{V}^{\top}-V\Lambda^{-2}V^{\top}X^{2}\widehat{V}\widehat{\Lambda}^{-2}\widehat{V}^{\top}\bigr)\Diag(P\cdot\mathbf{1}_{n})\Big).

Similarly,

\displaystyle\tr\bigg(

\displaystyle\bigl(\widehat{V}\widehat{\Lambda}^{-2}\widehat{V}^{\top}-V\Lambda^{-2}V^{\top}\bigr)\,\Diag\bigl(({\widehat{P}}-P)\cdot\mathbf{1}_{n}\bigr)\bigg)

(196)

\displaystyle=\sum_{l\geq 1}\tr\Big(\bigl[V\Lambda^{-2}V^{\top}S_{1}(X)+S_{1}(X)V\Lambda^{-2}V^{\top}-V\Lambda^{-2}V^{\top}(XP+PX)V\Lambda^{-2}V^{\top}\bigr]\Diag(T_{l}(X)\cdot\mathbf{1}_{n})\Big)

\displaystyle+\sum_{l\geq 1}\tr\Big(S_{1}(X)\bigl(\widehat{V}\widehat{\Lambda}^{-2}\widehat{V}^{\top}-V\Lambda^{-2}V^{\top}\bigr)\Diag(T_{l}(X)\cdot\mathbf{1}_{n})\Big)

\displaystyle-\sum_{l\geq 1}\tr\Big(V\Lambda^{-2}V^{\top}(XP+PX)\bigl(\widehat{V}\widehat{\Lambda}^{-2}\widehat{V}^{\top}-V\Lambda^{-2}V^{\top}\bigr)\Diag(T_{l}(X)\cdot\mathbf{1}_{n})\Big)

\displaystyle+\sum_{l\geq 1}\tr\Big(\bigl(\sum_{j\geq 2}V\Lambda^{-2}V^{\top}S_{j}(X)+\sum_{j\geq 2}S_{j}(X)\widehat{V}\widehat{\Lambda}^{-2}\widehat{V}^{\top}-V\Lambda^{-2}V^{\top}X^{2}\widehat{V}\widehat{\Lambda}^{-2}\widehat{V}^{\top}\bigr)\Diag(T_{l}(X)\cdot\mathbf{1}_{n})\Big).

Finally,

\displaystyle\tr\left(V\Lambda^{-2}V^{\top}\,\Diag\bigl(({\widehat{P}}-P)\cdot\mathbf{1}_{n}\bigr)\right)=\sum_{l\geq 1}\tr\left(V\Lambda^{-2}V^{\top}\,\Diag(T_{l}(X)\cdot\mathbf{1}_{n})\right).

(197)

Thus, we have shown that

\displaystyle\mu_{1}^{(2)}-\widehat{\mu}_{1}^{(2)}

\displaystyle=\tr\left(V\Lambda^{-2}V^{\top}\,\Diag(P\cdot\mathbf{1}_{n})-\widehat{V}\widehat{\Lambda}^{-2}\widehat{V}^{\top}\,\Diag({\widehat{P}}\cdot\mathbf{1}_{n})\right)+o_{p}\left(\max\left(\frac{k}{n^{2}\rho_{n}^{2}},\frac{k\sqrt{\log n}}{n^{2}\rho_{n}^{3/2}}\right)\right).

(198)

As in the proofs of
Eqs.

42
and
40
, one can verify that all linear in
X
contributions of the first term in
Eq.

198
(expanded term-wise in
Eqs.

194
,
195
,
196
and
197
) are of order

O_{p}\left(\frac{k\log n}{n^{2}\rho_{n}^{3/2}}\right).

Next, we will find the quadratic contributions of the same trace term in
Eq.

198
.

I.5.2
Step 2: Concentration of order-two terms around their mean.

We now demonstrate that the combined contribution of the second-order terms in
Eq.

194
is of order
\max\left(\frac{k}{n^{2}\rho_{n}^{2}},\frac{k\sqrt{\log n}}{n^{2}\rho_{n}^{3/2}}\right)
. Recall the term-by-term expansions of
Eq.

194
provided in
Eqs.

195
,
196
and
197
. Focusing first on
Eq.

195
and isolating all terms that depend quadratically on
X
, we obtain:

\displaystyle\text{Term 1}

\displaystyle=\tr\Big(S_{1}(X)^{2}\,V\Lambda^{-2}V^{\top}\Diag(P\cdot\mathbf{1}_{n})\Big)-\tr\Big(S_{1}(X)V\Lambda^{-2}V^{\top}(PX+XP)V\Lambda^{-2}V^{\top}\Diag(P\cdot\mathbf{1}_{n})\Big)

(199)

\displaystyle+\tr\Big(S_{1}(X)V\Lambda^{-2}V^{\top}S_{1}(X)\Diag(P\cdot\mathbf{1}_{n})\Big)-\tr\Big(V\Lambda^{-2}V^{\top}(PX+XP)S_{1}(X)V\Lambda^{-2}V^{\top}\Diag(P\cdot\mathbf{1}_{n})\Big)

\displaystyle+\tr\Big(V\Lambda^{-2}V^{\top}(PX+XP)V\Lambda^{-2}V^{\top}(PX+XP)V\Lambda^{-2}V^{\top}\Diag(P\cdot\mathbf{1}_{n})\Big)

\displaystyle-\tr\Big(V\Lambda^{-2}V^{\top}(PX+XP)V\Lambda^{-2}V^{\top}S_{1}(X)\Diag(P\cdot\mathbf{1}_{n})\Big)

\displaystyle+\tr\Big(\bigl(V\Lambda^{-2}V^{\top}S_{2}(X)+S_{2}(X)V\Lambda^{-2}V^{\top}-V\Lambda^{-2}V^{\top}X^{2}V\Lambda^{-2}V^{\top}\bigr)\Diag(P\cdot\mathbf{1}_{n})\Big)

\displaystyle=\tr\Bigg(\Big(\beta^{\perp}X\beta^{-2}X\beta^{-2}-\beta^{\perp}X\beta^{-3}X\beta^{-1}+\beta^{\perp}X\beta^{-4}X\beta^{\perp}-\beta^{-1}X\beta^{\perp}X\beta^{-3}+\beta^{-2}X\beta^{-1}X\beta^{-1}

\displaystyle-\beta^{-2}X\beta^{-2}X\beta^{\perp}-\beta^{-2}X\beta^{\perp}X\beta^{-2}+\beta^{-1}X\beta^{-1}X\beta^{-2}+\beta^{-1}X\beta^{-2}X\beta^{-1}-\beta^{-1}X\beta^{-3}X\beta^{\perp}

\displaystyle+\beta^{-4}X\beta^{\perp}X\beta^{\perp}-\beta^{-3}X\beta^{\perp}X\beta^{-1}-\beta^{-3}X\beta^{-1}X\beta^{\perp}+\beta^{\perp}X\beta^{\perp}X\beta^{-4}-\beta^{\perp}X\beta^{-1}X\beta^{-3}\Big)\Diag(P\cdot\mathbf{1}_{n})\Bigg)

\displaystyle=\text{Term 0}+\tr\left(\beta^{-4}X\beta^{\perp}X\beta^{\perp}\Diag(P\cdot\mathbf{1}_{n})\right)+\tr\left(\beta^{\perp}X\beta^{\perp}X\beta^{-4}\Diag(P\cdot\mathbf{1}_{n})\right)

\displaystyle+\tr\left(\beta^{\perp}X\beta^{-4}X\beta^{\perp}\Diag(P\cdot\mathbf{1}_{n})\right),

where we group the remaining components into
Term 0
, defined as

\displaystyle\text{Term 0}\coloneqq\tr\Bigg(\Big(

\displaystyle\beta^{\perp}X\beta^{-2}X\beta^{-2}-\beta^{\perp}X\beta^{-3}X\beta^{-1}-\beta^{-1}X\beta^{\perp}X\beta^{-3}+\beta^{-2}X\beta^{-1}X\beta^{-1}-\beta^{-2}X\beta^{-2}X\beta^{\perp}

\displaystyle+\beta^{-1}X\beta^{-1}X\beta^{-2}+\beta^{-1}X\beta^{-2}X\beta^{-1}-\beta^{-1}X\beta^{-3}X\beta^{\perp}-\beta^{-3}X\beta^{\perp}X\beta^{-1}

\displaystyle-\beta^{-3}X\beta^{-1}X\beta^{\perp}-\beta^{\perp}X\beta^{-1}X\beta^{-3}-\beta^{-2}X\beta^{\perp}X\beta^{-2}\Big)\Diag(P\cdot\mathbf{1}_{n})\Bigg).

We now proceed to bound all terms in
Eq.

199
starting first with

\tr\left(\beta^{-4}X\beta^{\perp}X\beta^{\perp}\Diag(P\cdot\mathbf{1}_{n})\right)=\sum_{j,k,l,m}w^{(1)}_{jklm}\,X_{jk}X_{lm},

where the coefficients are defined as

\displaystyle w^{(1)}_{jklm}=\sum_{i,q}(\beta^{-4})_{ij}(\beta^{\perp})_{kl}(\beta^{\perp})_{mi}P_{iq}.

Under
1
,
3
and
4
, these coefficients satisfy the bounds

\bigl|w^{(1)}_{jklm}\bigr|\lesssim\begin{cases}\dfrac{k^{2}}{n^{4}\rho_{n}^{3}},&k=l,\\[10.0pt]
\dfrac{k^{2}}{n^{5}\rho_{n}^{3}},&k\neq l.\end{cases}

Define

\displaystyle E_{1}=\mathbb{E}\left(\tr\left(\beta^{-4}X\beta^{\perp}X\beta^{\perp}\Diag(P\cdot\mathbf{1}_{n})\right)\right).

(200)

Consequently, it is straightforward to verify that the expectation satisfies

\displaystyle\big|E_{1}\big|\lesssim\frac{k^{2}}{n^{2}\rho_{n}^{2}}.

Next, we determine the asymptotic order of the corresponding variance:

\displaystyle\Var\left(\sum_{j,k,l,m}w^{(2)}_{jklm}X_{jk}X_{lm}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j,k,l,m}\left(w^{(2)}_{jklm}\right)^{2}\Var\left(X_{jk}X_{lm}\right)+\sum_{(j,k,l,m)\neq\atop(j^{\prime},k^{\prime},l^{\prime},m^{\prime})}w^{(2)}_{jklm}w^{(2)}_{j^{\prime}k^{\prime}l^{\prime}m^{\prime}}\Cov\left(X_{jk}X_{lm},X_{j^{\prime}k^{\prime}}X_{l^{\prime}m^{\prime}}\right)

(201)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j,k,l,m\atop k\neq l}\left(w^{(2)}_{jklm}\right)^{2}\Var\left(X_{jk}X_{lm}\right)+\sum_{j,k,l,m\atop k=l}\left(w^{(2)}_{jklm}\right)^{2}\Var\left(X_{jk}X_{lm}\right)

\displaystyle+\sum_{(j,k,l,m)\neq\atop(j^{\prime},k^{\prime},l^{\prime},m^{\prime})}w^{(2)}_{jklm}w^{(2)}_{j^{\prime}k^{\prime}l^{\prime}m^{\prime}}\Cov\left(X_{jk}X_{lm},X_{j^{\prime}k^{\prime}}X_{l^{\prime}m^{\prime}}\right)

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{k^{4}}{n^{5}\rho_{n}^{4}}.

Therefore, an application of Chebyshev’s inequality yields that, for some constant
C_{1}>0
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\left|\tr\left(\beta^{-4}X\beta^{\perp}X\beta^{\perp}\Diag(P\cdot\mathbf{1}_{n})\right)-E_{1}\right|\leq\frac{k^{2}}{n^{5/2}\rho_{n}^{2}}\right)\geq C_{1}.

(202)

By analogous reasoning as in
Eq.

202
, there exists a constant
C_{2}>0
such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\left|\tr\left(\beta^{\perp}X\beta^{\perp}X\beta^{-4}\Diag(P\cdot\mathbf{1}_{n})\right)-E_{2}\right|\leq\frac{k^{2}}{n^{5/2}\rho_{n}^{2}}\right)\geq C_{2},

(203)

where

\displaystyle E_{2}=\mathbb{E}\left(\tr\left(\beta^{\perp}X\beta^{\perp}X\beta^{-4}\Diag(P\cdot\mathbf{1}_{n})\right)\right).

(204)

Next, we will bound the term
\tr\left(\beta^{\perp}X\beta^{-4}X\beta^{\perp}\Diag(P\cdot\mathbf{1}_{n})\right)
, where we we again do our routine decomposition:

\tr\left(\beta^{\perp}X\beta^{-4}X\beta^{\perp}\Diag(P\cdot\mathbf{1}_{n})\right)=\sum_{j,k,l,m}w^{(4)}_{jklm}\,X_{jk}X_{lm}.

The coefficients are defined as

\displaystyle w^{(3)}_{jklm}=\sum_{i,q}(\beta^{\perp})_{ij}(\beta^{-4})_{kl}(\beta^{\perp})_{mi}P_{iq}.

Under
1
,
3
and
4
,

\bigl|w^{(3)}_{jklm}\bigr|\lesssim\begin{cases}\dfrac{k^{2}}{n^{4}\rho_{n}^{3}},&j=m,\\[10.0pt]
\dfrac{k^{2}}{n^{5}\rho_{n}^{3}},&j\neq m.\end{cases}

By analogous reasoning as in
Eq.

202
, there exists a constant
C_{3}>0
such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\left|\tr\left(\beta^{\perp}X\beta^{-4}X\beta^{\perp}\Diag(P\cdot\mathbf{1}_{n})\right)-E_{3}\right|\lesssim\frac{k^{2}}{n^{5/2}\rho_{n}^{2}}\right)\geq C_{3},

(205)

where

\displaystyle E_{3}=\mathbb{E}\left(\tr\left(\beta^{\perp}X\beta^{-4}X\beta^{\perp}\Diag(P\cdot\mathbf{1}_{n})\right)\right).

(206)

Identical asymptotic bounds hold for Term 0 in
Eq.

199
. Suppose we take
\tr\left(\beta^{\perp}X\beta^{-3}X\beta^{-1}\Diag(P\mathbf{1}_{n})\right)
as a representative term. Expanding the term, we get

\tr\left(\beta^{\perp}X\beta^{-3}X\beta^{-1}\Diag(P\mathbf{1}_{n})\right)=\sum_{j,u,v,l}w^{(0)}_{juvl}X_{ju}X_{vl},

where
w^{(0)}_{juvl}=\sum_{i,s}(\beta^{\perp})_{ij}(\beta^{-3})_{uv}(\beta^{-1})_{li}P_{is}
. We can check that under
1
,
3
and
4
,

\bigl|w^{(0)}_{juvl}\bigr|\lesssim\frac{k^{2}}{n^{5}\rho_{n}^{3}}.

Thus,

\left|\mathbb{E}\left[\tr\left(\beta^{\perp}X\beta^{-3}X\beta^{-1}\Diag(P\mathbf{1}_{n})\right)\right]\right|=O\left(\frac{k^{2}}{n^{3}\rho_{n}^{2}}\right),

and, proceeding similarly to
Eq.

201
, we have

\Var\left(\tr\left(\beta^{\perp}X\beta^{-3}X\beta^{-1}\Diag(P\mathbf{1}_{n})\right)\right)=O\left(\max\left(\frac{k^{2}}{n^{3}\rho_{n}^{2}},\frac{k^{2}}{n^{4}\rho_{n}^{5/2}}\right)\right).

Applying Chebyshev’s inequality, we obtain

\mathbb{P}\left(\big|\text{Term 0}\big|\lesssim\frac{k^{2}}{n^{3}\rho_{n}^{2}}\right)\geq C_{4}

(207)

for some constant
C_{4}>0
.
Therefore, combining the decomposition of
Eq.

199
with the concentration bounds established in
Eqs.

202
,
203
,
205
and
207
, there exists a constant
C_{5}>0
such that

\mathbb{P}\left(\big|\text{Term 1}-(E_{1}+E_{2}+E_{3})\big|\ll\frac{k^{2}}{n^{2}\rho_{n}^{2}}\right)\geq C_{5}.

(208)

Next, we move on to the second-order contributions from
Eq.

196
:

\displaystyle\text{Term 2}

\displaystyle=\tr\Big(\bigl[V\Lambda^{-2}V^{\top}S_{1}(X)+S_{1}(X)V\Lambda^{-2}V^{\top}-V\Lambda^{-2}V^{\top}(XP+PX)V\Lambda^{-2}V^{\top}\bigr]\Diag(T_{1}(X)\cdot\mathbf{1}_{n})\Big)

(209)

\displaystyle=\tr\Big(\bigl[\beta^{-3}X\beta^{\perp}+\beta^{\perp}X\beta^{-3}-\beta^{-2}X\beta^{-1}-\beta^{-1}X\beta^{-2}\bigr]\Diag(T_{1}(X)\cdot\mathbf{1}_{n})\Big).

Focusing on the first term of
Eq.

209
, we expand the trace as

\displaystyle\tr\left(\beta^{-3}X\beta^{\perp}\Diag(T_{1}(X)\cdot\mathbf{1}_{n})\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j,k,l}(\beta^{-3})_{ij}(\beta^{\perp})_{ki}X_{jk}(T_{1}(X))_{il}

(210)

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{i,j,k,m,l}(\beta^{-3})_{ij}(\beta^{\perp})_{ki}(VV^{\top})_{im}X_{jk}X_{ml}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{j,k,m,l}\underbrace{\left(\sum_{i}(\beta^{-3})_{ij}(\beta^{\perp})_{ki}(VV^{\top})_{im}\right)}_{w^{(4)}_{jklm}}X_{jk}X_{ml}.

Under
1
,
3
and
4
, it follows that
w^{(4)}_{jklm}\lesssim\frac{k^{2}}{n^{2}(n\rho_{n})^{3}}
. Consequently, it is straightforward to verify the expectation

\left|\mathbb{E}\left(\sum_{j,k,m,l}w^{(4)}_{jklm}X_{jk}X_{ml}\right)\right|\lesssim\frac{k^{2}}{n^{3}\rho_{n}^{2}}.

Next, we determine the order of the corresponding variance:

\displaystyle\Var\left(\sum_{j,k,m,l}w^{(4)}_{jklm}X_{jk}X_{ml}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j,k,m,l}\left(w^{(4)}_{jklm}\right)^{2}\Var\left(X_{jk}X_{ml}\right)+\sum_{(j,k,m,l)\neq\atop(j^{\prime},k^{\prime},m^{\prime},l^{\prime})}w^{(4)}_{jklm}w^{(4)}_{j^{\prime}k^{\prime}l^{\prime}m^{\prime}}\Cov\left(X_{jk}X_{ml},X_{j^{\prime}k^{\prime}}X_{m^{\prime}l^{\prime}}\right)

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{k^{4}}{n^{6}\rho_{n}^{4}}.

Therefore, an application of Chebyshev’s inequality to
Eq.

210
implies that with constant probability

\displaystyle\big|\tr\left(\beta^{-3}X\beta^{\perp}\Diag(T_{1}(X)\cdot\mathbf{1}_{n})\right)\big|\lesssim\frac{k^{2}}{n^{3}\rho_{n}^{2}}.

(211)

By analogous reasoning, identical asymptotic bounds hold for the remaining components in
Eq.

209
, which ultimately yields

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\big|\text{Term 2}\big|\lesssim\frac{k^{2}}{n^{3}\rho_{n}^{2}}\right)\geq C_{6}.

(212)

for some constant
C_{6}>0
.

Finally, the sole second-order contribution from
Eq.

197
is given by

\text{Term 3}=\tr\left(\beta^{-2}\Diag(T_{2}(X)\cdot\mathbf{1}_{n})\right).

Invoking
Lemma

13
, we obtain

T_{2}(X)=\bigl(\beta^{\perp}X\beta^{\perp}X\beta^{-1}+\beta^{-1}X\beta^{\perp}X\beta^{\perp}+\beta^{\perp}X\beta^{-1}X\beta^{\perp}\bigr),

which implies that

\text{Term 3}=\sum_{a,b,c,d}w^{(5)}_{abcd}\,X_{ab}X_{cd},

where the coefficients are defined as

\displaystyle w^{(5)}_{abcd}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j}(\beta^{-2})_{ii}\Big[(\beta^{\perp})_{ia}(\beta^{\perp})_{bc}(\beta^{-1})_{dj}+(\beta^{\perp})_{ia}(\beta^{-1})_{bc}(\beta^{\perp})_{dj}+(\beta^{-1})_{ia}(\beta^{\perp})_{bc}(\beta^{\perp})_{dj}\Big].

Under
1
,
3
and
4
, these coefficients satisfy the bounds

\bigl|w^{(5)}_{abcd}\bigr|\lesssim\begin{cases}\dfrac{k^{2}}{n^{4}\rho_{n}^{3}},&b=c,\\[10.0pt]
\dfrac{k^{2}}{n^{5}\rho_{n}^{3}},&b\neq c.\end{cases}

Define

\displaystyle E_{4}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left(\tr\left(\beta^{-2}\Diag(T_{2}(X)\cdot\mathbf{1}_{n})\right)\right)

(213)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left(\tr\left(\beta^{-2}\Diag(\left(\beta^{\perp}X\beta^{\perp}X\beta^{-1}+\beta^{-1}X\beta^{\perp}X\beta^{\perp}+\beta^{\perp}X\beta^{-1}X\beta^{\perp}\right)\cdot\mathbf{1}_{n})\right)\right).

Consequently, it is straightforward to verify that the expectation satisfies

\left|E_{4}\right|=\left|\mathbb{E}\left(\sum_{a,b,c,d}w^{(5)}_{abcd}X_{ab}X_{cd}\right)\right|\lesssim\frac{k^{2}}{n^{2}\rho_{n}^{2}}.

Next, we determine the asymptotic order of the corresponding variance:

\displaystyle\Var\left(\sum_{a,b,c,d}w^{(5)}_{abcd}X_{ab}X_{cd}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{a,b,c,d}\left(w^{(5)}_{abcd}\right)^{2}\Var\left(X_{ab}X_{cd}\right)+\sum_{(a,b,c,d)\neq\atop(a^{\prime},b^{\prime},c^{\prime},d^{\prime})}w^{(5)}_{abcd}w^{(5)}_{a^{\prime}b^{\prime}c^{\prime}d^{\prime}}\Cov\left(X_{ab}X_{cd},X_{a^{\prime}b^{\prime}}X_{c^{\prime}d^{\prime}}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{a,b,c,d\atop b\neq c}\left(w^{(5)}_{abcd}\right)^{2}\Var\left(X_{ab}X_{cd}\right)+\sum_{a,b,c,d\atop b=c}\left(w^{(5)}_{abcd}\right)^{2}\Var\left(X_{ab}X_{cd}\right)

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{(a,b,c,d)\neq\atop(a^{\prime},b^{\prime},c^{\prime},d^{\prime})}w^{(5)}_{abcd}w^{(5)}_{a^{\prime}b^{\prime}c^{\prime}d^{\prime}}\Cov\left(X_{ab}X_{cd},X_{a^{\prime}b^{\prime}}X_{c^{\prime}d^{\prime}}\right)

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{k^{4}}{n^{5}\rho_{n}^{4}}.

Therefore, an application of Chebyshev’s inequality yields that, for some constant
C_{7}>0
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\left|\text{Term 3}-E_{4}\right|\leq\frac{k^{2}}{n^{5/2}\rho_{n}^{2}}\right)\geq C_{7}.

(214)

Combining all the second-order terms from
Eqs.

208
,
212
and
214
, we conclude that there exists a constant
C_{8}>0
such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\left|\text{Term 4}-E\right|\ll\frac{k^{2}}{n^{2}\rho_{n}^{2}}\right)\geq C_{8},

(215)

where
\text{Term 4}=\text{Term 1}-\text{Term 2}-\text{Term 3}
contains all the quadratic terms of
X
in
Eq.

194
, and
E=E_{1}+E_{2}+E_{3}-E_{4}
as defined earlier in
Eqs.

200
,
204
,
206
and
213
.

Combining these arguments, we have therefore shown that with at least constant probability,

\displaystyle\mu_{1}^{(2)}-\widehat{\mu}_{1}^{(2)}

\displaystyle=E_{1}+E_{2}+E_{3}-E_{4}+(\text{terms of degree $\geq 3$ in $X$})

(216)

\displaystyle\quad+o_{p}\left(\max\left(\frac{k}{n^{2}\rho_{n}^{2}},\frac{k\sqrt{\log n}}{n^{2}\rho_{n}^{3/2}}\right)\right).

I.5.3
Step 3: Computing the order of order-two mean.

Using the identity

\mathbb{E}\left(X\beta^{\perp}X\right)=\Sigma\circ\beta^{\perp}+\Diag\left(\left(\Sigma-\Diag\left(\Sigma\right)\right)\mathrm{diag}\left(\beta^{\perp}\right)\right),

we can evaluate
E_{1},E_{2},E_{3}
, and
E_{4}
:

\displaystyle E_{1}

\displaystyle=\tr\left(\beta^{-4}\left[\Sigma\circ\beta^{\perp}+\Diag\left(\left(\Sigma-\Diag\left(\Sigma\right)\right)\mathrm{diag}\left(\beta^{\perp}\right)\right)\right]\beta^{\perp}\Diag\left(P\cdot\mathbf{1}_{n}\right)\right),

(217)

\displaystyle E_{2}

\displaystyle=\tr\left(\beta^{\perp}\left[\Sigma\circ\beta^{\perp}+\Diag\left(\left(\Sigma-\Diag\left(\Sigma\right)\right)\mathrm{diag}\left(\beta^{\perp}\right)\right)\right]\beta^{-4}\Diag\left(P\cdot\mathbf{1}_{n}\right)\right),

\displaystyle E_{3}

\displaystyle=\tr\left(\beta^{\perp}\left[\Sigma\circ\beta^{-4}+\Diag\left(\left(\Sigma-\Diag\left(\Sigma\right)\right)\mathrm{diag}\left(\beta^{-4}\right)\right)\right]\beta^{\perp}\Diag\left(P\cdot\mathbf{1}_{n}\right)\right),

and

\displaystyle E_{4}

\displaystyle=\tr\left(\beta^{-2}\Diag\left(\beta^{\perp}\left[\Sigma\circ\beta^{\perp}+\Diag\left(\left(\Sigma-\Diag\left(\Sigma\right)\right)\mathrm{diag}(\beta^{\perp})\right)\right]\beta^{-1}\cdot\mathbf{1}_{n}\right)\right)

(218)

\displaystyle+\tr\left(\beta^{-2}\Diag\left(\beta^{\perp}\left[\Sigma\circ\beta^{-1}+\Diag\left(\left(\Sigma-\Diag\left(\Sigma\right)\right)\mathrm{diag}(\beta^{-1})\right)\right]\beta^{\perp}\cdot\mathbf{1}_{n}\right)\right)

\displaystyle+\tr\left(\beta^{-2}\Diag\left(\beta^{-1}\left[\Sigma\circ\beta^{\perp}+\Diag\left(\left(\Sigma-\Diag\left(\Sigma\right)\right)\mathrm{diag}(\beta^{\perp})\right)\right]\beta^{\perp}\cdot\mathbf{1}_{n}\right)\right).

For the specific case of the SBM family, we show that
E_{1}=E_{3}=E_{4}=0
and
E_{2}\asymp\frac{k}{n^{2}\rho_{n}^{2}}
.

Calculation for
E_{1},\,E_{2}
:

Here we prove that
\beta^{\perp}\Diag\left(P\cdot\mathbf{1}_{n}\right)=\Diag\left(P\cdot\mathbf{1}_{n}\right)\beta^{\perp}
. We first analyze the structure of
\beta^{\perp}
under the SBM:

\beta^{\perp}=I-VV^{\top}=I-Z(Z^{\top}Z)^{-1}Z^{\top}.

Thus,
\beta^{\perp}
is a block-diagonal matrix with the
m
th block given by
(\beta^{\perp})^{(m)}=I_{n_{m}}-\frac{1}{n_{m}}J_{n_{m}}
, where
n_{m}
is the size of the
m
th community. Similarly, we have

\displaystyle\Diag(P\cdot\mathbf{1}_{n})=\Diag(ZBZ^{\top}\mathbf{1}_{n})=\Diag(Z\widetilde{d}),

(219)

where
\widetilde{d}=BZ^{\top}\mathbf{1}_{n}
is a
k\times 1
vector with elements
\widetilde{d}_{m}
for
m=1,\dots,k
. Thus,
Eq.

219
implies that
\Diag(P\cdot\mathbf{1}_{n})
also has a block structure, with the
m
th block being
\Diag(P\cdot\mathbf{1}_{n})^{(m)}=\widetilde{d}_{m}I_{n_{m}}
. Since both
\beta^{\perp}
and
\Diag(P\cdot\mathbf{1}_{n})
are block-diagonal matrices with matching block sizes, their product is also block-diagonal. The
m
th block of
\beta^{\perp}\Diag\left(P\cdot\mathbf{1}_{n}\right)
is therefore
\widetilde{d}_{m}\left(I_{n_{m}}-\frac{1}{n_{m}}J_{n_{m}}\right)
, which is identical to the
m
th block of
\Diag\left(P\cdot\mathbf{1}_{n}\right)\beta^{\perp}
. This proves that
\beta^{\perp}\Diag\left(P\cdot\mathbf{1}_{n}\right)=\Diag\left(P\cdot\mathbf{1}_{n}\right)\beta^{\perp}
.

Consequently, for
E_{1}
, we obtain

\displaystyle E_{1}

\displaystyle=\tr\left(\beta^{-4}\left[\Sigma\circ\beta^{\perp}+\Diag\left(\left(\Sigma-\Diag\left(\Sigma\right)\right)\mathrm{diag}\left(\beta^{\perp}\right)\right)\right]\beta^{\perp}\Diag\left(P\cdot\mathbf{1}_{n}\right)\right)

(220)

\displaystyle=\tr\left(\left[\Sigma\circ\beta^{\perp}+\Diag\left(\left(\Sigma-\Diag\left(\Sigma\right)\right)\mathrm{diag}\left(\beta^{\perp}\right)\right)\right]\beta^{\perp}\Diag\left(P\cdot\mathbf{1}_{n}\right)\beta^{-4}\right)

\displaystyle=\tr\left(\left[\Sigma\circ\beta^{\perp}+\Diag\left(\left(\Sigma-\Diag\left(\Sigma\right)\right)\mathrm{diag}\left(\beta^{\perp}\right)\right)\right]\Diag\left(P\cdot\mathbf{1}_{n}\right)\beta^{\perp}\beta^{-4}\right)

\displaystyle=0.

The exact same argument applies to
E_{2}
as well.

Calculation for
E_{4}
:

For the SBM, since
Z\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k}=\mathbf{1}_{n}
, we have
\mathbf{1}_{n}\in\col(Z)=\col(V)
because the block matrix
B
has full rank. This directly forces the second and third terms of
Eq.

218
to vanish since
\beta^{\perp}\cdot\mathbf{1}_{n}=0
.

Next, we evaluate the first term:

\tr\left(\beta^{-2}\Diag\left(\beta^{\perp}\left[\Sigma\circ\beta^{\perp}+\Diag\left(\left(\Sigma-\Diag\left(\Sigma\right)\right)\mathrm{diag}(\beta^{\perp})\right)\right]\beta^{-1}\cdot\mathbf{1}_{n}\right)\right).

Our target is to show that the components
\Diag\left(\beta^{\perp}\left[\Sigma\circ\beta^{\perp}\right]\beta^{-1}\cdot\mathbf{1}_{n}\right)
,
\Diag\left(\beta^{\perp}\left[\Diag\left(\Sigma\,\mathrm{diag}(\beta^{\perp})\right)\right]\beta^{-1}\cdot\mathbf{1}_{n}\right)
, and
\Diag\left(\beta^{\perp}\left[\Diag\left(\Diag\left(\Sigma\right)\mathrm{diag}(\beta^{\perp})\right)\right]\beta^{-1}\cdot\mathbf{1}_{n}\right)
vanish separately.

We established in the calculation for
E_{1}
that
(\beta^{\perp})^{(m)}=I_{n_{m}}-\frac{1}{n_{m}}J_{n_{m}}
. The matrix
\Sigma=P\circ(J_{n}-P)
also possesses a block structure. Thus,
\Sigma\circ\beta^{\perp}
is a block-diagonal matrix with the
m
th block given by
(\Sigma\circ\beta^{\perp})^{(m)}=P_{mm}(1-P_{mm})\left(I_{n_{m}}-\frac{1}{n_{m}}J_{n_{m}}\right)
. Furthermore,
\beta^{-1}\cdot\mathbf{1}_{n}=V\Lambda^{-1}V^{\top}\cdot\mathbf{1}_{n}\in\col(Z)
is a block-constant vector, with
q_{m}
denoting the value of the
m
th block. Evaluating the
m
th block of the vector
\left[\Sigma\circ\beta^{\perp}\right]\beta^{-1}\cdot\mathbf{1}_{n}
, we find

\displaystyle\left(\left[\Sigma\circ\beta^{\perp}\right]\beta^{-1}\cdot\mathbf{1}_{n}\right)^{(m)}

\displaystyle=\left(\left[\Sigma\circ\beta^{\perp}\right]\right)^{(m)}q_{m}\mathbf{1}_{n_{m}}

(221)

\displaystyle=P_{mm}(1-P_{mm})q_{m}\left(I_{n_{m}}-\frac{1}{n_{m}}J_{n_{m}}\right)\mathbf{1}_{n_{m}}=0.

Thus,
Eq.

221
proves that
\left[\Sigma\circ\beta^{\perp}\right]\beta^{-1}\cdot\mathbf{1}_{n}=0
.

Next,
\mathrm{diag}(\beta^{\perp})
is a block-constant vector with its
m
th block equal to
(1-\frac{1}{n_{m}})
. Consequently,
\Sigma\,\mathrm{diag}(\beta^{\perp})
is also a block-constant vector with its
m
th block equal to
P_{mm}(1-P_{mm})(1-\frac{1}{n_{m}})
. This yields

\left(\Diag\left(\Sigma\,\mathrm{diag}(\beta^{\perp})\right)\right)^{(m)}=P_{mm}(1-P_{mm})(1-\frac{1}{n_{m}})I_{n_{m}}.

We then have

\displaystyle\left(\beta^{\perp}\left[\Diag\left(\Sigma\,\mathrm{diag}(\beta^{\perp})\right)\right]\beta^{-1}\cdot\mathbf{1}_{n}\right)^{(m)}

\displaystyle=(\beta^{\perp})^{(m)}\left(\Diag\left(\Sigma\,\mathrm{diag}(\beta^{\perp})\right)\right)^{(m)}\left(\beta^{-1}\cdot\mathbf{1}_{n}\right)^{(m)}

(222)

\displaystyle=P_{mm}(1-P_{mm})q_{m}\left(1-\frac{1}{n_{m}}\right)(\beta^{\perp})^{(m)}\mathbf{1}_{n_{m}}=0.

This proves that
\Diag\left(\beta^{\perp}\left[\Diag\left(\Sigma\,\mathrm{diag}(\beta^{\perp})\right)\right]\beta^{-1}\cdot\mathbf{1}_{n}\right)=0
. By a nearly identical argument, one can show that
\Diag\left(\beta^{\perp}\left[\Diag\left(\Diag\left(\Sigma\right)\mathrm{diag}(\beta^{\perp})\right)\right]\beta^{-1}\cdot\mathbf{1}_{n}\right)=0
. Combining this result with
Eqs.

221
and
222
confirms that
E_{4}=0
.

Calculation for
E_{3}
:

From
Eq.

206
, we know that

E_{3}=\mathbb{E}\left(\tr\left(\beta^{\perp}X\beta^{-4}X\beta^{\perp}\Diag(P\cdot\mathbf{1}_{n})\right)\right)=\mathbb{E}\left(\|\beta^{-2}X\beta^{\perp}\left(\Diag(P\cdot\mathbf{1}_{n})\right)^{1/2}\|_{F}^{2}\right)>0.

Now, we evaluate the exact asymptotic order of
E_{3}
. We already know that
\Sigma
is a block-constant matrix. Additionally,
\beta^{-4}=V\Lambda^{-4}V^{\top}\in\col(Z)
is also a block-constant matrix. Thus,
\Sigma\circ\beta^{-4}
is a block-constant matrix, meaning that
\col(\Sigma\circ\beta^{-4})\subseteq\col(Z)
. This implies that
\beta^{\perp}(\Sigma\circ\beta^{-4})=0
. Thus, from
Eq.

217
, we have

\displaystyle E_{3}

\displaystyle=\tr\left(\beta^{\perp}\left[\Diag\left(\left(\Sigma-\Diag\left(\Sigma\right)\right)\mathrm{diag}\left(\beta^{-4}\right)\right)\right]\beta^{\perp}\Diag\left(P\cdot\mathbf{1}_{n}\right)\right)

(223)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\Diag(P\cdot\mathbf{1}_{n}))_{ii}\cdot\left(\Diag\left(\left(\Sigma-\Diag\left(\Sigma\right)\right)\mathrm{diag}\left(\beta^{-4}\right)\right)\right)_{ii}\cdot(\beta^{\perp})_{ii}.

Now,

\displaystyle\left(\Diag\left(\left(\Sigma-\Diag\left(\Sigma\right)\right)\mathrm{diag}\left(\beta^{-4}\right)\right)\right)_{ii}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j\neq i}\Sigma_{ij}\beta_{jj}^{-4}

\displaystyle\asymp\rho_{n}\tr(\beta^{-4})

\displaystyle\asymp\frac{k}{n^{4}\rho_{n}^{3}}

and

(\Diag(P\cdot\mathbf{1}_{n}))_{ii}=\sum_{j}P_{ij}\asymp n\rho_{n}.

Thus,
Eq.

223
implies that
E_{3}\asymp\frac{k}{n^{2}\rho_{n}^{2}}
.

Consequently, we have established that under the SBM, the combined term
E=E_{1}+E_{2}+E_{3}-E_{4}\asymp\frac{k}{n^{2}\rho_{n}^{2}}
. Combining this result with
Eq.

216
yields

\displaystyle\mu_{1}^{(2)}-\widehat{\mu}_{1}^{(2)}

\displaystyle\asymp\frac{k}{n^{2}\rho_{n}^{2}}+(\text{terms of degree $\geq 3$ in $X$})+o_{p}\left(\max\left(\frac{k}{n^{2}\rho_{n}^{2}},\frac{k\sqrt{\log n}}{n^{2}\rho_{n}^{3/2}}\right)\right).

(224)

In the final step, we bound the remaining higher-order terms to complete the proof.

I.5.4
Step 4: Bounding higher-order terms and completing the proof.

To bound the higher-order terms (those with power of
X
greater than or equal to
3
), we begin with the final term in
Eq.

194
which has been expanded in
Eq.

197
. Using
Lemma

13
, we have

\displaystyle\tr\left(V\Lambda^{-2}V^{\top}\,\Diag(({\widehat{P}}-P)\cdot\mathbf{1}_{n})\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{l\geq 3}\tr\left(V\Lambda^{-2}V^{\top}\Diag(T_{l}(X)\cdot\mathbf{1}_{n})\right)

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{l\geq 3}\frac{k}{(n\rho_{n})^{2}}\left(\frac{4}{n\rho_{n}}\right)^{l/2}\,n\rho_{n}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{k}{(n\rho_{n})^{2.5}}.

To bound the remaining higher-order contributions from
Eq.

194
, we revisit the decomposition in
Eq.

195
and follow an argument entirely analogous to the proof of
Eq.

42
in
Section

G.4
. The sole distinction is that in
Section

G.4
, after applying Bernstein’s inequality to the linear terms, we established a high-probability bound for terms of degree two or higher in
X
; here, however, we restrict our attention to terms of degree three or higher. The remainder of the proof remains unchanged, yielding a bound of
O_{p}\left(\frac{k}{(n\rho_{n})^{2.5}}\right)
.
The higher-order terms in
Eq.

196
can be handled analogously using the same reasoning as in the proof of
Eq.

42
. Again, the only modification is that we now discard all terms of order
3
or higher in
X
. This gives the same bound,

O_{p}\left(\frac{k}{(n\rho_{n})^{2.5}}\right).

Combining the above bounds for terms of order
3
or higher in
X
expanded in
Eqs.

195
,
196
and
197
, we conclude that the total contribution of all terms with power of
X
greater than or equal to
3
in
Eq.

194
is

O_{p}\left(\frac{k}{(n\rho_{n})^{2.5}}\right)=o_{p}\left(\frac{k}{n^{2}\rho_{n}^{2}}\right).

Thus,
Eq.

224
now can be updated to conclude that

\displaystyle\big|\mu_{1}^{(2)}-\widehat{\mu}_{1}^{(2)}\big|

\displaystyle\asymp\frac{k}{n^{2}\rho_{n}^{2}}+o_{p}\left(\frac{k}{n^{2}\rho_{n}^{2}}\right)+o_{p}\left(\max\left(\frac{k}{n^{2}\rho_{n}^{2}},\frac{k\sqrt{\log n}}{n^{2}\rho_{n}^{3/2}}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\asymp\frac{k}{n^{2}\rho_{n}^{2}},

which completes the proof.
